*Querdenker*de{}|Etymology}From quer.|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˈkveːɐ̯ˌdɛŋkɐ/}Ⓓc/cd/De-Querdenker.ogg|Noun}(mask.)}#lateral thinker,maverick,contrarian}•synononym [Freigeist],[Provokateur]}#}Querdenker Furtmeier war zeit seines Lebens ein '''Querdenker''',er ließ sich weder menschlich  noch politisch oder philosophisch in Schablonen pressen.}#German politics anti-vaxxer,anti-masker gloss:member of a movement opposing vaccination and other government policies}#}Querdenker Am vergangenen Samstag standen die '''Querdenker''' wieder zu Tausenden auf den Straßen der Großstädte. ...:1.000 bis 2.000 Menschen demonstrierten in Dresden,3.000 in München,1.500 in Berlin,2.000 in Düsseldorf. Wieder standen rechtsextreme Bruderschaften neben Regenbogenfahnen-Trägern,wieder forderten die einen das Ende der "Corona-Diktatur" und die anderen schrieben "Frieden,Freiheit und Liebe" auf ihre Plakate.}#}Querdenker}Das Unwort des Jahres ist "'''Querdenker'''",das Jugendwort für 2021 lautet "cringe" ... Zum Unwort des Jahres wurde mit 2.361 von 11.111 abgegebenen Stimmen "'''Querdenker'''" gekürt. Dabei handelt es sich um eine ursprünglich positiv besetzte Bezeichnung für Personen,die unkonventionell denken. "Heute sind in dieser neuen Gruppe von '''Querdenkern''' jedoch überwiegend Corona-Leugner,Impfverweigerer und Verschwörungstheoretiker zu finden",hieß es in der Jury-Mitteilung.

*von gestern*de{}|Etymology}lit:of/from yesterday.|Adjective}#idiomatic outdated}•DVDs sind '''von gestern'''. Du brauchst Blu-Ray.}•DVDs are '''antiquated'''. You need Blu-ray.}#idiomatic born yesterday}•Ich bin doch nicht '''von gestern!'''}•I wasn't '''born yesterday!'''|Usage notes}The two senses are contradictory. Sense 1 is based on ''yesterday'' meaning “of the past” (and hence “obsolete”),whereas sense 2 is based on the same word meaning “recent” (and hence “inexperienced”). This may lead to some confusion,though rarely to actual misunderstandings.

*’*de{}|Symbol}’:}#Indicating the omission of letters.}#}In case of enclitic pronouns:''wenn es → wenn’s,gibt es → gibt’s''}#}qualifier:sometimes proscribed In case of merging of prepositions and articles:''auf dem → auf’m''}#}In case of omission of ''e'' in the present indicative:''ich gehe → ich geh’,wir/sie gehen → wir/sie geh’n''}#}qualifier:sometimes proscribed In case of the omission of ''e'' in the imperative singular:gehe du → geh’ du''}#}qualifier:archaic In case of the omission of ''e'' in the imperative plural:''gehet ihr → geh’t ihr''}#}qualifier:archaic In case of the omission of ''e'' in the past participle:''entdecket → entdeck’t,bezeuget → bezeug’t''}#}qualifier:archaic In case of the omission of ''e'' in the genitive case:''Gottes → Gott’s,Königes → König’s''|Usage notes}In many cases where letters are omitted,there are also spellings without an apostrophes (e.g. wenns,aufm,gehn,geh,geht,entdeckt).

Bönhase*Bonhase*de{}|Alternative forms}Böhnhase|Etymology}From Middle Low German bönhase,from Middle Low German tcat,from Middle Low German tloft,attic+from Middle Low German thare,the former being cognate of from German tstage. The word is first attested in the early 14th century as a personal name (and is thus probably even older). Since the 15th century it meant “illegally working craftsperson”. The sense “cat” is only attested later on,but there can be little doubt that it is the original meaning. Compare from German Dachhase,from English roof rabbit,etc.,which in fact must be loan translations of the Low German unless there were some ancient source for all of them. It was formerly supposed that the illegal workers were called  as a pun on from Ancient Greek tworker in an ignoble trade,whence later from German Banause,but this is highly unlikely since Greek studies were only just being revived in the 15th century. More probably these workers were simply compared to stray cats who holed up in lofts and fled when chased (as they commonly were by the members of the official guilds).|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˈbøːnˌhaːzə/}Ⓓ1/14/De-B%C3%B6nhase.ogg|Noun}(mask.)}#North Germany cat; roof rabbit}•synononym Dachhase}#North Germany A skilled manual worker or craftsperson who works illegally,originally without the concession of the respective guild.}#North Germany botcher; bungler gloss:incompetent worker}•synononym [Pfuscher],[Stümper]

*-chen*de{}|Alternative forms}-ken}-gen|Etymology}From Middle High German -echen,from Middle High German -ichen,from Old High German ⋆-ihīn,from Old High German ⋆-uhīn,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆-ukīn.}Native to the Central German dialects,''-chen'' has widely replaced the southern-based -lein in standard usage. Cognate with from German Low German -ken,from German Low German -ke as well as from Dutch -tje and from Dutch -ken. More at -kin,(English).|Pronunciation}IPA:çn̩}The use of a syllabic nasal IPAchar:n̩ is less common in this ending than otherwise. The vowel tends to be preserved and often fronted,yielding IPAchar:çɪn.}IPA:qqvariant,especially after fricatives like s. Never after a vowel or sonorant.}Ⓓ4/4e/De--chen.ogg|Suffix}-chen:}#nowadays the most common suffix to create a diminutive form}•Hund}•Backe}•Nicker|Usage notes}Words ending in the suffix ''-chen'' are always neuter,whatever the gender of the basic word.}Generally,with few exceptions,diminutives with ''-chen'' have an umlauted stem vowel. Double vowels must be singled as the combinations ''ää'' and ''öö'' are not permitted in modern German orthography (e.g. Härchen from Haar,Bötchen from Boot).}Word-final ''-e'' and ''-en'' are lost before the diminutive ending:Kärtchen from Karte,Gärtchen from Garten.}Words ending in ''-ch'' (and some others) use the double suffix -elchen,e.g. Strichelchen from Strich. However,the suffix -lein is more common for these words in writing (Strichlein).}The plural diminutive is generally unchanged,but some words with a plural in -er may carry this ending over to the plural diminutive,yielding -erchen. This is standard in Kindchen → Kinderchen; most other forms (like Lämmerchen,etc.) are slightly informal or dated.|Derived terms}suffixsee:de|See also}-lein}-erl}-i

*Elen*de{}|Alternative forms}obsolete|Etymology}From Middle High German eln,elent (mid-13th c.). Usually derived from Old Lithuanian elenis (“deer”,modern from Lithuanian élnias),from Proto-Indo-European ⋆h₁el-. This derivation is supported by the fact that the from Middle High German el(e)n form appears during a time when the elk was gradually dying out in the German-speaking lands. However,it must be noted that the Lithuanian word does not refer to the elk specifically. Even more importantly,from Middle High German elen (from Middle High German elhen by regular loss of prevocalic ''h'' in Central and Low German dialects) can represent a generalised oblique case or plural of from Proto-Germanic ⋆elhô,weak-stem variant of from Proto-Germanic ⋆elhaz. This would make it a mere byform of from German notext1.|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˈeːlən/|Noun}n:m)}#archaic moose,Eurasian elk gloss:species}•synononym Elch|Derived terms}Elenantilope}Elentier

*Frikadelle*de{}wikipedia:langde|Etymology}Late 17th century,Borrowed nl (attested 1607),itself from French fricadel(le). The French is only recorded in 1742,but its earlier existence is likely as it is a variant of from French fricandeau (1548),which see. A further relation with from Italian frittatella is speculative. Derivation of from German Frikadelle from Italian instead of Dutch (as suggested in some sources) is implausible in view of the chronology and regional distribution. Compare also from Dutch frikandel.|Pronunciation}IPA:ˌfʁiː-}Ⓓ3/38/De-Frikadelle.ogg|Noun}(fem.)}#regional frikadelle,rissole,fried meatball}•synononym Thesaurus:Frikadelle|Usage notes}is the most widely accepted word for this dish in German. It is nevertheless regionally bound and heard most often in central-western and north-western Germany.}The typically German way of preparing see at m:frikadelle. *Geschlecht*de{}|Alternative forms}Geschlächt|Etymology}From Middle High German geslechte,from Old High German gislahti,a collective formation from Old High German slahta,from Proto-Germanic ⋆slahtō,related to from Proto-Germanic tto strike,hit (DWDS).}Cognate with from Dutch geslacht,from Middle Low German geslechte,from Italian schiatta,from Polish szlachta. The Germanic root is that of Schlag,schlagen. The modern sense developed among this root's descendants first in Menschenschlag,in which the underlying meaning of ''Schlag'' is "strike of coinage".|Pronunciation}IPA:/ɡəˈʃlɛçt/}Ⓓ6/64/De-Geschlecht.ogg}Rhymes:ɛçt|Noun}(neut.,genitive:Geschlechtses,plural:[Geschlechtereobsolete])}#sex}#grammar gender}#}Geschlecht Die Deutsche Sprache ordnet,wie jede andere,ihre Substantive nach gewissen '''Geschlechtern''',deren wir drei haben:1) das männliche (masculinum),2) das weibliche (femininum),3) das sächliche (neutrum). Kommen die beiden ersten '''Geschlechter''' einem Worte zugleich zu,so sagt man:sein '''Geschlecht''' ist ein Doppelgeschlecht oder ein gemeinschaftliches (commune); kommen ihm alle drei zu,so ist es generis omnis,oder ein Allgeschlecht.}:translationLike every other language,German orders its nouns into certain '''genders''',of which we have three:1) the masculine (masculinum),2) the feminine (femininum),3) the neuter (neutrum). Where both of the first two '''genders''' apply equally to a word,its '''gender''' is called a double-gender or a common (commune); where all three apply,it is called generis omnis,or an all-gender.}#sex organ}#genus,type,race}#lineage,generation,family}#dynasty,house|Synonyms}sense:grammatical gender Genus,Wortgeschlecht}sense:genital organ Genital,Geschlechtsorgan,Geschlechtsteil}sense:genus Gattung,Genus}sense:dynasty Dynastie,Haus|Hyponyms}Adelsgeschlecht}Menschengeschlecht}Naturgeschlecht}Sachgeschlecht}Sprachgeschlecht}Wortgeschlecht|Derived terms}geschlechtlich}eingeschlechtig

*Halve Hahn*de{}|Etymology}Borrowed ksh. The origin is possibly an order like Can I get half a sandwich?,the last two words of which were then re-interpreted (either by foreigners or jokingly) as ''halve Hahn''.|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˌhalvəˈhaːn/|Noun}Halve Hahn:}#regional a cheese sandwich,cut in half|Usage notes}The expression is rare in actual usage. Even in thick Ripuarian dialect one generally uses the plain and simple word m:Kiesbrütche ("cheese sandwich"). However,''Halve Hahn'' is occasionally used as a shibboleth among Rhineland people and can be found on the menus of traditional Colognian pubs (as a humorous knowledge test for foreigners).}The term is either uninflected,e.g. (accusative) ''den Halve Hahn'',or the first part behaves like a normal German adjective,thus ''den Halve'''n''' Hahn'',and so on.

*heben*de{}|Etymology}From Middle High German heben,from Middle High German heven (rarer from Middle High German heffen),from Old High German heffen,from Old High German heven,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆habbjan. Compare from Dutch heffen,from English heave,from Danish hæve. from German kapieren.}The ''-b-'' is regular in the past tense and participle. In Middle High German the paradigm was regularised by spreading ''-b-'' to the present tense; reinforced in East Central German by the local shift ''-v-'' → ''-b-'' (as in from East Central German Oben for Ofen). The fricative is preserved in related Hefe. The original past forms hub,gehaben were retained until Early Modern German. The verb was then shifted to the class of fliegen etc. (Compare from Dutch heffen,which was also shifted,though to a different ablaut class.)|Pronunciation}IPA:ˈheːbm̩}Ⓓ1/14/De-heben.ogg}Ⓓ3/39/De-heben2.ogg|Verb}heben(hob:hubarchaic,gehoben,höbe:hübearchaic)}#transitive to lift; to raise}#transitive to heave; to hoist}#reflexive to rise; to lift|Derived terms}abheben}anheben}aufheben}ausheben}beheben}darunterheben}drüberheben}einheben}emporheben}entheben}erheben}Gewichtheben}Hebebühne}Heben}heraufheben}herausheben}herüberheben}herunterheben}hervorheben}hinaufheben}hinausheben}hineinheben}hinüberheben}hinwegheben}hochheben}überheben}überheblich}umheben}unterheben}verheben}wegheben}zurückheben

Hoffmann|urlhttps://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/17362/pg17362-images.html|passageDaß ich niemals Bohnenkönig geworden,daß ich im Paar oder Unpaar immer falsch geraten,daß mein Butterbrot immer auf die fette Seite gefallen,von allem diesen Jammer will ich gar nicht reden:aber ist es nicht ein schreckliches Verhängnis,daß ich,als ich denn doch nun dem Satan zum Trotz Student geworden war,ein '''Kümmeltürke''' sein und bleiben mußte?|See also}Deutschtürke

In order to avoid ambiguity,"to let" can be expressed as ''jemandem erlauben,etwas zu tun'' or ''zulassen,dass jemand etwas tut'',while "to make" can be expressed as ''jemanden dazu bringen,etwas zu tun'' or ''dafür sorgen,dass jemand etwas tut''.}In perfect tenses,''lassen''+infinitive takes a double-infinitive construction,i.e. both verbs are in the infinitive:''Ich habe mir die Haare schneiden lassen.'' (“I’ve had my hair cut.”). However,colloquially,''gelassen'' may also be used.|Derived terms}ablassen}alleinlassen}anlassen}auflassen}auslassen}beiseitelassen}belassen}bleiben lassen}bleibenlassen}dalassen}davonlassen}dortlassen}durchlassen}einlassen}entlassen}erlassen}fortlassen}herablassen}heranlassen}herauflassen}herauslassen}hereinlassen}herlassen}herüberlassen}herunterlassen}hierlassen}hinablassen}hinauflassen}hinauslassen}hindurchlassen}hineinlassen}hinlassen}hinterlassen}hinüberlassen}hinunterlassen}lockerlassen}loslassen}nachlassen}niederlassen}ranlassen}rauflassen}rauslassen}rüberlassen}runterlassen}überlassen}unterlassen}verlassen}Verschwindenlassen}vorbeilassen}vorüberlassen}weglassen}weiterlassen}zerlassen}zufriedenlassen}zulassen}zurücklassen}zusammenlassen

*-lein*de{}|Alternative forms}-el}-li}-lin}-elein|Etymology}From Middle High German -elīn,from Middle High German -līn,from Old High German -ilī,from Old High German -ilīn,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆-ilīn. There is a limited number of other forms that remain in common use,but for the most part  now has a poetic and archaic tone. The general diminutive suffix in the written language and in northern speech is -chen,while southern informal use prefers regional suffixes like -le,-li (Swabian/Alemannic) and -erl (Austro-Bavarian).}Diminutives in  generally have umlaut. The endings  are all deleted before the suffix.}The plural remains unchanged. However,a few nouns with a plural in -er may carry the same ending over to the plural diminutive,and a few diminutives referring to people allow a plural in . This is common with Kindlein →  and Fräulein → . Most other such forms are rare.|Derived terms}suffixsee:de|See also}-chen}-erl}-i

to act as if one were (something,or a certain way) machen ( with  :auf(acc)a role)}•Jetzt '''macht''' er auf Elder Statesman.}•Now he's '''playing''' the elder statesman.}#intransitive to do one's business,do number two or number one,go gloss:to defecate or urinate}•groß '''machen'''}•'''to go''' poop}•qchildish}•klein '''machen'''}•'''to go''' pee}•qchildish}#reflexive to do,to fare machen ( with  adverbwell,badly,etc.)}•'''Machen''' Sie sich gut?}•Are you '''doing''' well?}#reflexive to look gloss:to have an appearance of being machen ( with  adjectivea particular way)}•Der Mantel '''macht''' sich sehr schön.}•The coat '''looks''' very nice.}#dative reflexive to get cracking (on/with),to get a move on (it),to get down (to) machen ( with  :an(acc)something)}#in the imperative come on,let's go|Usage notes}Unlike the English distinction between ''make'' and ''do'',the verb machen can be used as a synonym for tun in most cases. However,''tun'' cannot be used for the proper senses of ''machen''.}The verb ''machen'' can be combined with many different nouns to create various expressions. These can often be translated into English with make or take. For many of these expressions,a more specific verb is available and is usually preferred in formal or written language.}:Angaben machen}:eine Aufzeichnung machen}:Ausflüchte machen}:Bankrott/Pleite machen}:eine Besorgung machen}:ein Bett machen}:Feierabend machen}:Ferien machen}:ein Feuer machen}:ein Foto machen}:Fortschritte machen}:Gewinn machen}:Kasse machen}:einen Krach machen}:einen Kurs machen}:Notizen machen}:Platz machen}:eine Reise machen}:Schluss machen}:Spaß machen}:Überstunden machen}:Witze machen|Derived terms}abmachen}anmachen}aufmachen}ausmachen}aus einer Mücke einen Elefanten machen}ausmachen}beimachen}bekanntmachen}sich davonmachen}draufmachen}durchmachen}einmachen}fertigmachen}sich fortmachen}freimachen}heilmachen}hermachen}herummachen}heruntermachen}hinmachen}kaputtmachen}kleinmachen}losmachen}miesmachen}mitmachen}nachmachen}niedermachen}sich ranmachen}reinemachen}rummachen}saubermachen}Sinn machen}übermachen}Übung macht den Meister}vermachen}vormachen}wegmachen}weitermachen}wiedergutmachen}zumachen}Middle High German|Etymology}goh,from from Proto-West Germanic ⋆makōn.|Pronunciation}gmh-IPA|Verb}gmh-verb}#to make}#to do|Descendants}Alemannic German:mache}:Alsatian:màche,(Alemannic German) north,màcha,(Alemannic German) south}:Swabian:macha}Bavarian:macha}:Cimbrian:machan}:Mòcheno:mòchen}Central Franconian:maache}Hunsrik:mache}Luxembourgish:maachen}East Central German:-}:Lusatian:machn,(East Central German)}:machn}:Vilamovian:maha}German:machen}machng (?)}Pennsylvania German:mache}Yiddish:מאַכן

röntgen*rontgen*de{}|Etymology}From the noun Röntgen,with -en reinterpreted as the infinitive ending of a verb.|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˈrœntçən/}Ⓓ3/3e/De-r%C3%B6ntgen.ogg|Verb}de-verb}#to X-ray}•Gepäckstück '''geröntgt'''. Gepäckstück nicht geöffnet.}•Piece of baggage X-rayed. Piece of baggage not opened. (Tag found on baggage on returning from Germany.)|Usage notes}The unusual stop cluster in this word is often simplified to having a fricative instead,producing a pronunciation like IPAchar:ʁœntç-. This simplification is not limited to those (northern and central German) accents that feature or featured a merger of ''g'' and ''ch'' in initial or coda position. In the dialects which do feature a merger of final ''-g'' and ''-ch'',this pronunciation of ''röntgen'' is also not necessarily limited to forms where the cluster is in the coda.

*Schanze*de{}|Etymology}Of from German nocap1 origin. Attested since the 15th century. Several etymologies have been proposed.}Pfeifer suggests the regional Franconian/Hessian term from Rhine Franconian tbasket (itself of unknown origin) as the origin,arguing that entrenchments resemble baskets from the inside,being dug-out earth pits that have been reinforced from the inside with brushwood. He rejects a derivation from posn,plural of from Italian scanso,claiming that such theories are based on an unverifiable meaning of “defense”/“resistance” of the Italian term.}See also from Dutch schans,from Danish skanse,from Swedish skans and from English sconce.|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˈʃantsə/}Ⓓ9/97/De-Schanze.ogg}Rhymes:antsə|Noun}(fem.)}#entrenchment,redoubt}•synononym Schanzanlage}•Verteidigungsanlage}#ramp; jump,(English) gloss:an installation,either temporary or permanent,that is driven over quickly (with skis,a bike etc.) so that the driver is propelled into the air}•synononym Sprungschanze}•Skischanze|Derived terms}sense:ramp Schanzenrekord|Descendants}desctree:bor1

*schon*de{}|Etymology 1}From Middle High German tbeautifully; appropriately; completely,from Old High German tbeautifully,adverb of from Old High German scōni,whence Cognate German tbeautiful.  For sense development from "''beautiful''" to "''indeed,surely; already''" compare from English pretty.|Pronunciation}IPA:aastandard}De-schon.ogg(Germany)}De-schon2.ogg(Germany)}IPA:aaalternatively in central and southern Germany and in Austria}De-at-schon.ogg(Austria)}Rhymes:oːnɔn|Adverb}#already gloss:happening rather early,continuing rather long}•Es ist erst 11 Uhr und '''schon''' über 30 Grad im Schatten.}•It’s only 11 a.m. and '''already''' more than 30 degrees (Celsius}•°C) in the shade.}•Er ist '''schon''' seit drei Wochen krank.}•He’s been sick for three weeks '''already.'''}#previously,before gloss:at some time in the past; ''but not'':before a certain event}•Ich erinnere mich,dass wir solche Fälle '''schon''' hatten.}•I remember that we had such cases '''before.'''}#expresses that an action has been completed; already but more frequent; yet in questions}•Wir sind spät dran,aber diesen Stapel haben wir immerhin '''schon''' bearbeitet.}•We’re running late,but at least we’ve ('''already''') finished this pile.}•Hast du dir '''schon''' die Zähne geputzt?}•Have you brushed your teeth '''yet?'''}#modal particle expresses a weighing of arguments,contrast between two statements,partial agreement,concession; admittedly; sure(ly); definitely; indeed}•Man sollte Kinder nicht bestrafen. — Man sollte Kinder '''schon''' bestrafen,nur eben sinnvoll.}•One shouldn’t punish children. — One should '''definitely''' punish children,but wisely.}•Niedrige Steuern heben den Konsum. — Das ist '''schon''' richtig,aber...}•Low taxes increase consumption. — That is '''admittedly''' true,but...}•Du merkst doch auch,dass ich Recht habe? — Ja,'''schon.'''}•You do see I’m right,don’t you? — Yeah,'''I guess'''.}#really (as an intensifier)}•Das ist '''schon''' etwas merkwürdig.}•That's '''really''' a little strange.|Usage notes}In order to emphasize ''schon'' in the sense of “already”,another fitting adverb of time is usually added:“Are you done already?” → ''Bist du jetzt schon fertig?'' Stressing ''schon'' itself is also possible but less common. In some other senses,however,''schon'' is commonly stressed.|Synonyms}sense:yet bereits}sense:surely doch,ja,jawohl,wirklich|Antonyms}antsense:already erst|Derived terms}schon mal|Pronunciation}IPA:/ʃoːn/}Rhymes:oːn|Verb}#verb form of [schonen]}#colloquial verb form of [schonen]

*Sekt*de{}|Alternative forms}obsolete|Etymology}Borrowed in the 17th century,and provided with a paragogic ''-t'',from fr,which is also the original sense in German. The sense “sparkling wine” is believed to originate from an anecdote in 19th-century Berlin. The actor Ludwig Devrient supposedly ordered a bottle of wine using the phrase “from German Bring er mir Sekt,Schurke!”,based on the German translation of the line “Give me a cup of sack,(English),rogue!” from Shakespeare’s ''Henry IV''. He was served sparkling wine,his usual order,and this sense was given to the word  when the phrase and anecdote caught on.|Pronunciation}IPA:/zɛkt/}Ⓓ7/79/De-Sekt.ogg}säckt |Noun}(mask.,plural:[Sekt(e)s],diminituve:[Sektchen])}#sparkling wine defdate:from 19th c.}•synononym [[[[Schaumwein (formal; officialese)],[;]],[Prickelbrause  (colloquial; jokingly)]],[Puffbrause (derogatory)]],[Nuttenbrause (derogatory)]}#}Sekt Was in diesen Wein- und Sektgütern passiert,ist ein Wandel von genügsamen und oft auch etwas zuckrig schmeckenden '''Sekten''' der Vergangenheit hin zu feinen,eleganten,oft knochentrockenen,frischen und druckvollen '''Sekten''',die plötzlich international Aufsehen erregen.}#obsolete sack gl:light-colored dry wine from southern Europe,especially Spain defdate:from 17th c.}•synononym Südwein}#}Sekt Gib mir ein Glas '''Sekt''',Schurke! – Ist keine Tugend mehr auf Erden?}•Give me a cup of '''sack''',rogue! Is there no virtue extant?|Derived terms}Jahrgangssekt}Natursekt}Sektfrühstück}Sektglas}Sektkorken}Sektgut}Sektkellerei}Sektlaune|See also}Champagner}Schampus

*sich*de{}|Etymology}From Middle High German sich,from Old High German sih,from Proto-Germanic ⋆sek. Compare from Yiddish זיך,from Dutch zich.}This pronoun was originally restricted to the accusative case,while simple personal pronouns were used in the dative. Dative use of from Middle High German sich in from Middle High German - was restricted to northern dialects of Central German. In Early Modern German,a rare dative sir also occurred,formed by analogy with mir,dir. An obstacle to the generalisation of this form was the use of  in the plural,where there operated the conflicting analogy with the merged accusative/dative forms uns,euch.|Pronunciation}IPA:/zɪç/}Ⓓe/ea/De-sich.ogg}Ⓓ0/08/De-sich2.ogg}Rhymes:ɪç|Pronoun}sich:}#reflexive pronoun Reflexive pronoun of the third person singular:herself,himself,themself,itself,oneself (''direct'' or ''indirect object'')}#reflexive pronoun Reflexive pronoun of the third person plural:themselves (''direct'' or ''indirect object'')

*Sitz im Leben*de{}|Etymology}Coined by Hermann Gunkel in 1930. lit:seat in life.|Noun}Sitz<m.sg> im Leben)}#idiomatic sociological setting|Usage notes}Though sometimes rendered by phrases like "life setting","situation in life" etc. the German term ''Sitz im Leben'' is better translated by "sociological setting" or left untranslated. As a technical term,it refers to the social setting of a text,particularly in the Bible. It is primarily used today in academic contexts,especially theology and biblical studies.

The ''-h-'' was introduced into the spelling by analogy with sehen,in which it had become mute but was retained in spelling.}Past forms are inherited from Old High German stuond,from Old High German stuonden,etc. These are themselves suppletive of Proto-West Germanic from Proto-West Germanic ⋆standan's past forms in ''}stōnd-'',replacing former forms inherited from Proto-West Germanic ⋆stān in ''}stōþ-''. Compare english stood. Their a-vocalism is due to analogy with other strong verbs such as binden (''band'') and finden (''fand''),dating back to Middle-High German. *Straßenbahn*de{}|Alternative forms}de-CH-alt|Etymology}From Straße.|Pronunciation}IPA:ˈʃtraːsənˌbaːn}Ⓓ2/24/De-Stra%C3%9Fenbahn.ogg|Noun}(fem.)}#streetcar,tramway}•synononym [[[[[[[[[Elektrische (dated)],[;]],[Tram (Austria]],[ Switzerland)]],[;]],[Trambahn]],[;]],[Tramway (Austria)]],[;]],[Straba (Saxony — Würzburg)]|Usage notes}An urban train might drive on the streets on paths that are also used by cars,or it might move in tracks separated from the rest of the road traffic in an open-air area called Gleiskörper,and it might drive underground in tunnels. It depends on the city whether the very same trains drive on all three tracks or whether there are different trains for the three types of tracks. In the latter case,as in Berlin,one w:distinguishes the three types by name,calling the one driving with the cars ''Straßenbahn'',the one on exclusive tracks S-Bahn,the ones serving the subway U-Bahn. Whereas in Cologne,(English) there is an indistinct Straßenbahn,U-Bahn or w:Stadtbahn,the w:S-Bahn being a means of transportation of greater distance to connect the whole urban area with its multiple cities. But in places where there isn’t such a tripartite system and a single train moves between tracks used by cars,exclusive s-train-tracks and tunnels in a single route,it is called Straßenbahn indistinctly,or also Stadtbahn,this last especially by the officials themselves,so in Bielefeld,the terms S-Bahn and U-Bahn being uncommon,the city being w:surrounded by rural areas otherwise only having busses for local traffic. Whereas in Hamburg “w:Straßenbahn” is a historical term since the Straßenbahnen proper have been abolished completely by 1978.|Descendants}only in Germany itself

*Topf*de{}ein '''Topf'''|Etymology}From Middle High German topf,from Old High German ⋆topf,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆dopp,from Proto-Germanic thollow,bowl,from Proto-Indo-European tdeep,hollow. Cognate with from Lukpa tlittle cup,thimble,from Low German tshell,pod,bowl,from Saterland Frisian tshell,pod,bowl,from Lukpa tcup in which a diamond is cut.}Both in historic sources and modern dialects,the word is almost entirely restricted to East Central German (generally as from East Central German Topp,in the very south as from East Central German Topf). West Central German only shows a neuter derivative in from Middle High German düppen (whence e.g. from Luxembourgish Dëppen). Nevertheless it must have existed in Old High German as the a-mutation assures inheritance. Beginning with Luther's Bible it established itself as the general standard term for “pot”.|Pronunciation}IPA:tɔp͡ɸ}Ⓓe/ee/De-Topf.ogg|Noun}(mask.,genitive:Topfes,plural:[Topfe],diminituve:Topf)}#pot gloss:flat-bottomed,open-topped vessel,especially for cooking food}•synononym [(northern)Pott],[(southern)Hafen]|Hyponyms}Blumentopf}Farbtopf}Honigtopf}Kochtopf}Nachttopf}Pflanztopf}Römertopf}Rumtopf}Übertopf|Derived terms}töpfern}Töpfer

*viel*de{}|Alternative forms}Viel|Etymology}from Proto-Indo-European ⋆pleh₁-}From Middle High German vile,from Old High German many,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆felu,from Proto-Germanic ⋆felu,from Proto-Indo-European ⋆pleh₁-. More at from English fele.|Pronunciation}IPA:/fiːl/}Ⓓb/bb/De-viel.ogg}Rhymes:iːl}fiel|Pronoun}viel:}#much,a lot}•Es ist '''viel''' passiert.}•'''Much''' has happened.|Usage notes}Compare the similar,but semantically different pronoun vieles.}See also the pronoun mehr.|Determiner}viel:}#much,many}•Er hat '''viel''' Geld verloren.}•He lost '''a lot of''' money.|Usage notes}In the singular,the adjective is usually left unchanged when it is not preceded by an article or determiner (see example sentence above). Otherwise it is declined like a normal adjective:''das viel'''e''' Geld''.}In the plural,the adjective is usually declined even without a preceding article or determiner:''viele Kinder''. However,it may be left unchanged when modified by a preceding adverb,e.g. in the combinations wie ''viel'' (“how many”) and so ''viel'' (“so many”):''wie viel Kinder'' or ''wie viele Kinder''.}The comparative form mehr is invariable and never declined; it cannot be preceded by any article or determiner (note however mehrere,mehreres,and obsolete mehre,mehres). The superlative meist is declined like a normal adjective.}<!--|Adverb}de-adv:meisten}#much,a lot}•Wir haben '''viel''' gelacht.}•We laughed a lot.|Derived terms}allzuviel}soviel}wieviel|See also}manch}Pennsylvania German|Etymology}from Proto-Indo-European ⋆pleh₁-}From Middle High German vile,from Old High German many,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆felu,from Proto-Germanic ⋆felu,from Proto-Indo-European ⋆pleh₁-. *werden*de{}|Etymology}From Middle High German wërden,from Old High German werdan,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆werþan,from Proto-Germanic ⋆werþaną. Cognate with from Dutch worden,obsolete from English worth,from Swedish varda,from Norwegian Nynorsk verta,and also with from Latin to turn.}The use as a passive auxiliary is old and found throughout West Germanic, whereas the use as a future auxiliary is a Middle High German innovation. It originated in inchoative constructions with the present participle:. Since the 14th century,the participle was increasingly replaced with the infinitive,probably by analogy with the older future auxiliaries wollen and sollen. These latter were displaced by  in Modern German,but survive dialectally.|Pronunciation}IPA:-dən}Ⓓb/b4/De-werden.ogg}Ⓓc/c1/De-werden3.ogg|Verb}(irreg,auxiliary:sein)}#geworden will; to be going to; forms the future tense werden ( with  bare&infdo something)}•Ich '''werde''' nach Hause gehen.}•I '''will''' go home.}#auxiliary would; forms the subjunctive tense of most verbs werden ( with  bare&infdo something)}#worden to be done; forms the passive voice werden ( with  past&part)}•Das Buch '''wird''' gerade gelesen.}•qpresent tense}•The book '''is''' being read.}•Ich '''wurde''' in Europa geboren.}•qpreterite tense}•I '''was''' born in Europe.}•Er war geschlagen '''worden'''.}•qpast perfect tense}•He had '''been''' beaten.}#geworden to become; to get; to grow; to turn}•Es '''wird''' heißer.}•It's '''getting''' hotter.}#for one to begin or come to feel or experience werden ( with  datfor someone+predicate&adjectivea certain state)}•'''Usage:''' In this sense '''werden''' is always conjugated in the third person singular and takes a dative noun. The impersonal subject '''es''' may be present,but is often taken as implied. *lauter*de{}|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˈlaʊ̯tɐ/|Etymology 1}From Middle High German tclean,clear,pure,from Old High German hlūtar,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆hlūtr.|Adjective}#formal sincere; honorable; of integrity; correct}•synononym [aufrichtig],[ehrenhaft]}•Der Angeklagten können '''lautere''' Absichten nicht abgesprochen werden.}•The defendant’s '''honorable''' intentions cannot be denied.}#formal genuine; pure}•synononym [eitel],[echt]}•Der König trug einen Ring aus '''lauterem''' Gold.}•The king wore a ring of '''genuine''' gold.}#colloquial a lot of; a bunch of; much; many; several}•synononym [[[[eine Menge],[etliche]],[einige]],[viel]],[alle möglichen]}•Ich hab auf der Party '''lauter''' alte Freunde getroffen.}•I met '''a bunch of''' old friends at the party.|Derived terms}Lauterkeit}läutern}unlauter|Adverb}#formal in a sincere,honorable,correct manner; with integrity}•Die andere Seite hat sich in den Verhandlungen nicht '''lauter''' verhalten.}•The other side did not behave '''correctly''' during the negotiations.}#colloquial just; only; exclusively; ''often best translated with'' all}•Die Leute in der Verwaltung sind '''lauter''' Idioten.}•Those people in the administration are '''all''' idiots.}•Das ist doch '''lauter''' Unsinn,was du sagst.}•But that’s '''just''' nonsense what you’re saying.|Usage notes}It is somewhat arbitrary to separate the sense “a lot,a bunch” (see the adjective) from the sense “exclusively,only” (adverb). Both often overlap and are not explicitly distinct in their construction. The distinction ''uninflected,(English) adjective,(English)'' versus ''adverb,(English)'' has been chosen here for simplicity,that is because the German synonyms and English translations tend to be of the respective parts of speech. — It may be well possible to analyse both senses as either adjectives or adverbs.|Synonyms}ausschließlich}lediglich}nur}alles|Adjective}lauter:}#comparative of [laut]|Adjective}lauter

*Zettel*de{}ein ''Zettel''.|Pronunciation}IPA:ˈt͡sɛtl̩}Zet+tel|Etymology 1}Early Modern High German zeddel,zedel,from Middle High German zedele,from Middle High German zedel,a loan from Italian cedola,from German cedula,from Latin schedula,the diminutive of from Latin scheda,from Latin tstrip of papyrus ultimately from Ancient Greek tsplinter,fragment. Cognate to from Low German Zeddel,Palatine from Rhine Franconian Zeddel,from English schedule.}The spelling with ''-tt-'' was found from the 15th century in Upper German; the spelling with ''-dd-'' persisted until the 19th century,primarily in authors from Central or Northern Germany. Luther mostly writes ''zedel'',Goethe alternates between ''zeddel'' and ''zettel''. The (now obsolete) spelling variant ''zettul'' is influenced by from French cédule.}Occasional weak inflection was found in Middle High German and persisted into the 18th century. The original feminine gender was retained until Luther's time,but occasional masculine or neuter gender was found by the late Middle High German period. The masculine gender has predominated since the 19th century.|Alternative forms}zedel}zeddel}ziddel}zöttel}zättel}zettul}Zeddel}Zettul|Noun}(mask.,diminituve:[Zettelchen])}#a small or loose piece of paper,slip}#}Zettel der schutzengel ... sträuet etliche '''zettele''' nider,darauf schöne sprüch aus heiliger schrift wider die hoffart}•the guardian angel scatters a number of loose pages from above,on which are fine sayings from the holy writ against the sin of pride}#note,message,letter}#}'''1511''',Albrecht von Eyb,''Spiegel der Sitten'',Augsburg,https://books.google.ch/books?idsaNJAAAAcAAJ 92v. (O 2b):}#}: name er sollich '''zedel''' der klagen,verprennet sy und sprach+he Constantine took such letters of complaint against the bishops,burned them,and said ...}#}Zettel Gut. Ich bekam einen '''Zettel''':ich sollte um ein Uhr in der Salpêtrère sein. Ich war dort.}#poster,placard,public notice}#}'''1725''',Chr. Thomasius,''Gedanken und Erinnerungen'',Marburg,3,104:}#}:ohnerachtet herr d. Pf. meinen '''zettul''' vom schwartzen brette hatte abreiszen lassen+nevertheless Mr. d. Pf. had my poster taken down from the university's bulletin board|Derived terms}Beipackzettel}Denkzettel}Handzettel}Kassenzettel}Klebezettel}Lohnzettel}Lottozettel}Meldezettel}Merkzettel}Notizzettel}Packzettel}Schmierzettel}Schummelzettel}Spickzettel}Totenzettel}Waschzettel}Wunschzettel}Zettelkasten}Zettelwirtschaft|Etymology 2}A technical term in weaving recorded from the later 15th century (in Middle High German in the compound from Middle High German zettelgarn).}The noun is derived from the verb zetten via the suffix -el denoting tools (as in Hebel,Stößel). The verb ''zetten'' itself is from Proto-Germanic tto strew,scatter,whence also from English tdung.}In German translations of Shakespeare's ''A Midsummer Night's Dream'' the name of the weaver Nick Bottom (one of the “rude mechanicals,” from glossball or skein of thread replaced by a German technical term of weaving following Christoph Martin Wieland,1762).|Noun}(mask.)}#weaving warp}•synononym [Kettfaden],[Werft]}#}Zettel kainen '''zettel''' innemen oder wúrken,er hab in dann selbs gezettlet}•he should take over or work no warp,unless that he has himself warped it}#}Zettel wenn ... an einem kleid eines aussatzes maal sein wirt,es sey ... am '''zettel''' oder am eintrag|tThe garment also that the plague of leprosy is in,whether ... it be in the warp,or woof ...}#}Zettel In den Niederlanden hatte das Teppichwirken mit stehendem '''Zettel''',Hautelisse genannt,sich schon auf den höchsten Grad erhoben.|Derived terms}zetteln

The second part in all cases is from Proto-Germanic truler. Compare also from Hungarian Emre.|Proper noun}(mask.,plural:[Emmerichswith an article])}#Given Name|Etymology 3}Mainly from the given name (etymology 2),less often from the town (etymology 1).|Proper noun}m:f,s:-with an article,s:-,genitive:Emmerichswith an article,plural:[Emmerichs])}#usagechiefly western German

*Ungeziefer*de{}|Etymology}From early modern German ,,a variant form of from Middle High German ungezibere. These pertain to from Old High German sacrificial animal and hence originally meant “animals unsuitable for sacrifice”,ultimately from Proto-Germanic offering,sacrifice,victim. The word is rarely attested in medieval texts due to suppression of words reminiscent of heathen practices,but must have survived in lower registers.|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˈʊŋɡə-/}Ⓓ9/9d/De-Ungeziefer.ogg|Noun}(neut.)}#uncountable vermin; pests; ''restricted to small animals,usually ''insects'' or ''spiders,'' sometimes also ''mice'',etc.''}•Schädling}•Der Küchenschrank saß voller Motten und dieses '''Ungeziefer''' war in alle Dosen und Tüten eingedrungen.}•The kitchen cupboard was full of moths and these '''vermin''' had got into all containers and bags.}#countable a vermin animal; a despised creature}#}Ungeziefer :translatorw:Willa Muir|translation_year1933|passageAls Gregor Samsa eines Morgens aus unruhigen Träumen erwachte,fand er sich in seinem Bett zu einem ungeheuren '''Ungeziefer''' verwandelt.|translationAs Gregor Samsa awoke one morning from uneasy dreams he found himself transformed in his bed into an enormous '''insect'''.

*Schweizer*de{}|Alternative forms}Schweitzer|Etymology}from Middle High German swīzer. Equivalent to Schweiz+er. The senses "doorman" and "milker" arose because Swiss people were commonly employed in Germany in those professions; the sense "sacristan" arose because sacristans' garb resembled that of the Swiss Guards. For the sense development compare also from French Swiss:from French doorman.|Pronunciation}IPA:/ˈʃvaɪ̯t͡sɐ/}Ⓓ6/69/De-Schweizer.ogg}Rhymes:aɪ̯t͡sɐ}Schwei+zer|Noun}(mask.) feminine form:Swiss (female) or melker (female)}#Swiss person,Swiss (male)}•synononym Eidgenosse}•Mitteleuropäer}#Swiss Guard}•Schweizergarde}#Catholicism sacristan}•synononym Küster}#milker gl:a person or man who milks cows}#doorman|Hypernyms}Mitteleuropäer|Hyponyms}Auslandsschweizer}Deutschschweizer}Innerschweizer}Nordschweizer}Ostschweizer}Papierschweizer}Südschweizer}Welschschweizer}Westschweizer}Zentralschweizer}Aargauer}Baseler}Berner}Genfer}Graubündner}Luzerner}Neuenburger}Tessiner}Thurgauer}Zürcher|Derived terms}Schweizerin}schweizerisch|Descendants}obsolete}desctree:bor1|Proper noun}surname)}#surname:de|Adjective}#relational of Switzerland|Usage notes}U:de:-er words|Derived terms}Schweizerheit
